                                                                                 Carlos Drummond de Andrade
--- seg-81c3d7eba5cb54d59290d316 [needs-review] ---
Love,

I'm sad because I am the only living Brazilian who has never seen a flying saucer. In my street, everyone saw them and spoke with their crew in a mixed language of Carioca and green luminescent signals that anyone understands, right? They boarded (as guests) and flew around, beyond, without needing a passport or tax clearance certificate, without dollars, love, without dollars. They returned full of news and superiority. They look at me with benevolent disdain. I am the outcast, the one who only sees a truck, a cinema poster, a street hole, and other pedestrian evidence. A friend I have every week goes to see your saucer on Itaipu Beach. This one doesn't say anything to me, verbally, but his eyes! They tell of prodigies made so common by their weekly occurrence that they are only kept secret from those who are not capable of listening to and understanding a saucer. Why does the UFO refuse to reveal itself to me alone? Perhaps so that the acronym doesn't get lost, for after all, nothing is more identified than a flying saucer present today in São Paulo, Bahia, Barra da Tijuca, and Barra Mansa. (The shepherds of this village already make fun of me as I search, in vain, for __UFO_PROTECTED_000__.)

===== PAGE 9 [page-469cf4ec321113d5996aa263] =====
--- seg-dfcc3aa0b6c0f7df171c3149 [needs-review] ---
Registration Process for intangible property: "The E.T. Case and the Varginha Identity"

Municipality of Varginha | MG

Year 2023 | Exercise 2025
--- seg-dc3da05bdd32f8a4e5ab601a [needs-review] ---
The 1988 Brazilian Federal Constitution, in Article 216, defines Brazilian cultural heritage as "material and immaterial goods, taken individually or collectively, that bear reference to the identity, actions, and memories of the different groups that form Brazilian society." Memory, among other attributes, is a crucial element in the defense and preservation of goods selected and designated as Brazilian intangible cultural heritage. In the case of the dossier presented here, the asset concerns collective memory. Memory is always abstract and relates to a community, a place, a group of people, stemming from individual memories that are disseminated and recreated through social interaction. It is within the collective of experiences and lived memories that they are disseminated and can be mythified.

Pierre Nora defined collective memory as "the recollection or set of recollections, conscious or not, of an experienced and/or mythified event by a living community whose sense of the past is an integral part of its identity" (Collective Memory, Dictionary of New History, 1978, p. 398). In the case of the Varginha ET, presented in this dossier, there was a mythification of an experience lived by a limited group of people. This experience, regardless of its nature, was disseminated and transformed with great force into one of the identities of an entire municipality, a territory.

The concept of collective memory, as a sociological fact, was first introduced by Maurice Halbwachs (2013). For Halbwachs (2013), collective memory is formed in the context of the individual through a process of social interaction.
--- seg-9d4417a682354a3a4c240bb5 [machine-unreviewed] ---
For our memory to benefit from the memories of others, it is not enough for them to present their testimonies; it is also necessary that it has not diverged from their memories and that there are many points of contact between one and the other for the memory that reminds us to be reconstructed on a common basis. It is not enough to reconstruct piece by piece the image of a past event to obtain a memory. It is necessary that this reconstruction works from common data or notions that are in our spirit and also in that of others, because they are always passing from one to the other and vice versa, which will only be possible if they have been part of and continue to be part of the same society, the same group (HALBWACHS, 2013, p. 39).
--- seg-c2d430f9d164f27b575fdbc2 [needs-review] ---

===== PAGE 10 [page-a549248d1aa916f6d0f84879] =====
--- seg-a2cfb3abebb74b16474b060c [needs-review] ---

--- seg-a1a67552852d3fec001e359f [machine-unreviewed] ---
Although it was Halbwachs who brought the issue of collective memory to the forefront, it was Pierre Nora, with his *Les lieux de mémoire* (1984), that propelled historical studies to debate memory and collective memory. It was in this study that Nora defined the differences between memory and history.
--- seg-3ec23c6cb5cd63fea5a2f0b9 [machine-unreviewed] ---
Memory and history: far from being synonyms, we become aware that they are opposites. Memory is life, always carried by living groups, and in this sense, it is in permanent evolution, open to the dialectic of remembrance and forgetting, of the unconsciousness of its successive deformations, vulnerable to all uses and manipulations, susceptible to long latencies and sudden revitalizations. History is the always problematic and incomplete reconstruction of what no longer exists. Memory is a phenomenon always current, a place lived in the eternal present; history, a representation of the past. Because it is affective and magical, memory accommodates details that comfort it; it feeds on vague, telescopic, global or floating memories, particular or symbolic, sensitive to all transfers, scenes, censures or projections. History, as an intellectual and secularizing operation, demands critical analysis and discourse. Memory installs remembrance in the sacred; history liberates it, making it always prosaic. (NORA, 1984, p. XIX).
--- seg-23c5e9482dd7438be6187790 [needs-review] ---
In the case of intangible heritage, although historians and social scientists base studies on the records in the historical issues of goods, it is in the "affective and magical" memory that social groups linked to a particular intangible good are anchored and recreate their practices, collectively and daily. These recreations enable the continuity of the good. In the field of intangible heritage, only memories that are alive and latent within a community, that make sense to its holders, and that can be recreated and have continuity in the future, are of interest.

Memory and history are intertwined and both are inseparable, as Paul Ricœur and Jacques Le Goff taught us. Therefore, there is no competition between memory and history or a classification in terms of superiority or inferiority, but rather a circularity. They can sometimes be antagonistic, sometimes complementary, as modes of managing the past (Villas Boas, 2015).

The UNESCO, on the other hand, defines "intangible cultural heritage as practices, representations, expressions, knowledge, and techniques - together with instruments, objects,

--- seg-607838b3151a4983dd337208 [needs-review] ---
The registration is grounded in both the Brazilian Federal Constitution and the international guidelines of UNESCO, followed by Iphan, which advocate for "respecting the intangible cultural heritage of communities, groups, and individuals involved" and the understanding that communities themselves should recognize what constitutes their heritage.

The protection afforded by this registration, as rigorously described in this dossier, pertains to the representations created within the municipality surrounding the Varginha ET case. The essence of this protection lies in preserving the memories and narratives associated with this unique event.

===== PAGE 12 [page-963c302a781572125c486323] =====
--- seg-9b7d571f372e0381398771ab [needs-review] ---
Registration Process for intangible property: "The E.T. Case and the Varginha Identity"

Municipality of Varginha | MG

Year 2023 | Exercise 2025
--- seg-dd1bea3b7fc5602f99e765bc [needs-review] ---
The representation presented here is based on the definitions of Carlo Ginsburg and Roger Chartier, following UNESCO's understanding that representations, along with practices, expressions, knowledge, and techniques, can be recognized as Intangible Cultural Heritage. To this end, a new municipal register book, the Register Book of Representations, was opened to inscribe representations, as is the case with the Varginha ET.

Furthermore, one of the possibilities for recognizing a cultural heritage asset, both tangible and intangible, is through associations and representations made about it over time, through music, poetry, literature, images, souvenirs, cartoons, statues, monuments, buildings, paintings, magazine and newspaper publications, online and offline, among others. As demonstrated in this study, the Varginha ET case has hundreds of recreations and representations made since 1996 until today. This keeps the asset constantly alive in collective imagination and offers possibilities for recreation and continuity.

The symbolic values and images associated with an asset and that inspire distinct representations are recognized by UNESCO for the classification of an asset. One of the criteria emphasized by UNESCO for the recognition of assets and their inscription on the Representative Lists of the World Heritage is that the asset is directly or materially associated with living events or traditions, ideas, beliefs, or works of art and literature of exceptional universal significance. For example, in the Rio de Janeiro nomination as a cultural landscape, the associations and representations made of the "marvelous city" were highlighted at various moments in the dossier.

These associated values should project the culture and significant aspects of the place represented. When representations of what would be aliens appear in animations, cartoons, or books, having coffee and eating cheese bread, it refers to the local Varginha and Minas Gerais culture as a whole. These representations, indeed, make Varginha recognized through them. If a person is photographed under the space ship replica installed by COPASA in Varginha's center, it's not necessary for the person to describe their location to recognize that they are in Varginha. In other words, the representation, in this case, the simulacrum of the ship, takes the place of the represented thing, which in this case is Varginha and the relation with the ET's history.
--- seg-54cebfc701848a7d19ae3f13 [needs-review] ---

===== PAGE 13 [page-7ef8a4d36a2cab132e63f928] =====
--- seg-6a00992c36b16c4f8038096b [needs-review] ---
Registration Process for intangible property: "The E.T. Case and the Varginha Identity"

Municipality of Varginha | MG

Year 2023 | Exercise 2025
--- seg-afddfe98e853e584e49514d0 [needs-review] ---
The consistency of representations, discourses, and images created and recreated around the Varginha ET Case kept the memory of the event alive, even for those who never visited Varginha. In the national and global imagination, Varginha is the municipality where the strange creatures allegedly emerged in January 1996, identified by ufologists as extraterrestrial beings. In this sense, the ET is from Varginha, it is in Varginha, even if the supposed appearance event only occurred once. It's as if Varginha's territory belongs to the ETs, or at least those who allegedly appeared there. It is very likely that the mystery and secrecy imposed by the authorities, particularly the military, around the case fuels imagination, and new representations and narratives are continuously created over time.

Varginha became known worldwide as the city of the Varginha ET from what became known as the "Varginha Incident" or "Varginha Incident" or, alternatively, "Varginha ET Case," as it will be referred to in this Dossier.

The event that gave global visibility to the municipality of Varginha, located in the south of the state of Minas Gerais, occurred on January 20, 1996. That day, young Katia Andrade Xavier and her sisters Liliane de Fátima Silva and Valquíria Aparecida Silva reportedly saw a creature while crossing a vacant lot between the Andere Garden neighborhood and Santana in Varginha. Beside a wall, the three young women spotted a creature with brown, viscous skin as if oil had been applied to it, large red eyes, and a large head with three protuberances resembling horns. At the same time, residents of the surrounding areas of Varginha reported having seen unidentified flying objects (UFOs).

Reports of military and fire department movements were granted by several local people who saw the trucks and armed men blocking curious onlookers.

The Varginha ET Case was reported by the Brazilian and international press. Fantástico produced a special report in May 1996, exposing the case (available at: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=mItYpoUSJyY) and making it known nationally and internationally. Subsequently, several other journalistic groups visited Varginha to record and report the event.
--- seg-d02cf4aeedf481f3fee5e752 [needs-review] ---

===== PAGE 14 [page-527d2932ef62f9f943bbcc46] =====
--- seg-1a03008621429736457ec691 [needs-review] ---
Registration Process for intangible property: "The E.T. Case and the Varginha Identity"

Municipality of Varginha | MG

Year 2023 | Fiscal Year 2025
--- seg-fb0b80a6e5484520a461efd8 [needs-review] ---
Starting from the generating event, which at this moment does not matter to discuss its truth or falsehood, the municipality of Varginha became globally known due to the ET story and space ships. Replicas of the creature described by the three girls began to be produced, and large sculptures were installed in the central region of the municipality. Initially, there was a movement from the press and ufology, and later, the population of the municipality started to identify and recreate the story in various representations.

Thus, we identify that there was first an outside-in movement regarding the Varginha ET Case, and only later did the municipality itself recognize the narrative. Some citizens, like the artisan Mauri Bomfim, created the first representations in large statues and sculptures immediately after the event, in 1996. After him, dozens of people followed, producing diverse representations in crafts, buildings, urban furniture, sculptures, cartoons, drawings, among others.

With the disclosure and great impact of the case, the municipality "gained" an ET, as in all references to the alien, it is described as the Varginha ET. It is not an ET in Varginha or an ET that was in Varginha. But it is "of," a personal pronoun. The ET is recognized as being from Varginha, and even cartoons and playful videos refer to an ET who became a miner, wears boots, likes cheese bread, is friendly, and talks with the characteristic accent of the people from the interior of Minas Gerais. The representations of the ET that is said to be "of" Varginha appear stereotyped, with signs that refer to the people from the interior of Minas Gerais, whether by accent, clothing, or taste for coffee and cheese bread.

In the municipality of Varginha, the ET Case is materialized in various pieces of furniture, architectural constructions, crafts, illustrations, characters, sculptures, among others.

In 2003, COPASA began constructing a suspended water reservoir 20 meters above the ground, with a capacity of 100,000 liters of water, in Praça Marechal Floriano, located in the city center, in the shape of a UFO or space ship. Anyone who passes through Varginha, whether for leisure or work, takes a photo next to the "ship," as the construction became known. When the registration process was announced in the media, a person recounted that they had already deviated 90 kilometers from their route on a trip just to take a photo with the structure.

The photograph under the "ship" in Varginha. Other accounts corroborated this, stating that they
themselves or their acquaintances had done the same.

What compels a person to deviate kilometers from their original route just to be in Varginha's territory
to "see" a mockup of a space ship or statues resembling what one imagines to be an extraterrestrial? It is
Walter Benjamin who teaches us about the experience.

Experience, for Benjamin, is what gives authority to the elders. Experience only exists in experiencing
a fact, a place, a taste, a sensation, a music, among others. Experience is a human condition by
nature, therefore, it is cultural.

With the narratives and representations created around the Varginha ET Case, the city's identity was
constituted by adhering to the ET's history, moving away from its previous identification as coffee
and coffee cultivation. In a movement from the outside in and also from the inside out of the
municipality.

Research indicated that from the episode known as the "Varginha ET," the discourse about the
municipality changed. Initially, there was an external movement to attribute one or more
extraterrestrials to the municipality. Then, the local government and entrepreneurs appropriated the
theme and began reproducing local architectural elements with details that refer to the ET's
history.

Previously, up until the 1990s, Varginha referred to itself as the "Coffee Land." Even today,
coffee cultivation is strong in the municipality. To such an extent that there is a Dry Port to export
the coffee production not only of the city but of the entire South Mineiro region.

Starting in the 2000s, more precisely, local and external discourses to the municipality reinforced
the idea of the "Varginha ET," meaning that the ET became part of Varginha's identity, as if it were
a local entity. The ET is "from" Varginha.

Due to the diffuse nature of the asset, the Municipal Cultural Heritage Council of Varginha
decided to create a new Register Book: of Representations. Additionally, the asset was also inscribed
in the Register Book of Places, as the entire Varginha territory recognizes and is recognized as
related to the Varginha ET Case.

The methodology applied for the elaboration of this dossier was based on historical and ethnographic
research, oral interviews, and bibliographic research. To this end, a field work was conducted

The research team conducted a study in the municipality of Varginha between January and April 2023, with field research activities taking place in the urban area. The methodology combined field research with desk research, integrating data from various sources, including bibliographic and documentary research, to construct the narrative presented in this dossier. The study also adhered to the technical parameters required by the State Institute of Historical and Artistic Heritage of Minas Gerais (IEPHA/MG), following the normative deliberation of the State Cultural Heritage Council (CONEP).

This dossier therefore presents information regarding the historical data of the region and the object of preservation, along with a detailed description of the asset, the protection plan, photographic documentation, and the bibliographic and documentary references used. It also includes the bureaucratic documentation that registered the asset, "The E.T. Case and Varginha's Identity," as local intangible cultural heritage.

The Cultural Asset "The E.T. Case and Varginha's Identity," due to its representative, symbolic, identity-forming, and cultural values, which placed the municipality on the global ufology map, had its registration approved by the Municipal Cultural Heritage Council of Varginha during its 218th meeting on May 18, 2023. This decision was homologated by Municipal Decree No. 11628 on June 5, 2023. Administrative Process No. 8488/2023. The asset was registered in two books: the Book of Places Registration and the Book of Representations Registration. The asset is subject to special protection under Article 5, Paragraph 1, of Municipal Decree No. 8.__UFO_PROTECTED_01__, which establishes the registration of intangible cultural assets within the municipality of Varginha/MG, classifying it as "Varginha's Cultural Heritage."

2. Descriptive analysis of the cultural asset
--- seg-d8f786162e8fb49e0eba52eb [machine-unreviewed] ---
2.1 Cultural Heritage History
--- seg-9eb2c8ca973d82539d7ec664 [needs-review] ---
2.1 The Formation of South Minas Gerais
--- seg-1b72a2bdc3427c509da77892 [needs-review] ---
The southern region of Minas, where the municipality of Varginha was formed, is the portion of Minas demarcated by the Grande River and bordering the states of Rio de Janeiro and São Paulo through the Mantiqueira Mountains. It had a late occupation process compared to the central and mining region of the Province of Minas Gerais.

Official history tells of occupation based on the colonial explorer context, but we know that indigenous people inhabited all of Brazil, including the southern region of Minas, before the arrival of colonists and the extermination of indigenous populations.

In the early centuries of occupation, the entire region belonged to the Comarca do Rio das Mortes, with São João Del Rei as its seat. This southern Minas region was occupied primarily after the discovery of gold in the central Minas region and soon became a target of disputes between the Capitania of São Paulo and Minas. Geographically, it corresponds to "a vast area intersected by several paths that ensured access and enabled the commercial interconnection of the main slave-holding areas of the South-Central" (ANDRADE, 2005).

Regarding the occupation of the southern Minas land and the arrival of pioneers into the forests and prairies of the Verde River, Lefort (1950) reports:
--- seg-4a0ebcfcbda4e08e0fc10bfd [needs-review] ---
Since 1737, the South of Minas has been officially explored. Officially, due to the possessory act of Cipriano José da Rocha, the judge of the district of São João D'El Rei, during his reprisal visit to the quilombos (rebel communities) and the desertion of the precious mineral, which was sent to São Paulo (LEFORT, 1950).
--- seg-bd17bfcbff0027b02a15a0ca [machine-unreviewed] ---
The same author further cites:
--- seg-9f5cc53984a725b6631f96c0 [needs-review] ---
In a private capacity, for a long time, the mines of Rio Verde were known and explored by the Paulistas, who refrained from disclosing them to "the authorities of São Paulo or Minas. With the execution of the order of the Minas governor, Martinho de Mendonça, a winding trail was blazed from São João d'El Rei to the campaigns of Rio Verde, made by the Ouvidor and his entourage, who spared no effort in clearing forests and plains to open the road, which indeed became free for all.
--- seg-60203bec6aa358bdc794981f [needs-review] ---

A person, standing and on horseback. From São João d'El Rei, they crossed the ancient parish of Nossa Senhora da Conceição do Rio Grande and Carrancas, where a group of adventurers ventured into the forests and prairies adjacent to the Verde River. And they arrived at the current Campaign, where they saw a magnificent land, joining the coveted mineral found in all the streams and rivers (LEFORT, 1950).
--- seg-9fee411c67cce7bfdf915b05 [needs-review] ---
According to Lenharo (1993), the South of Minas Gerais constituted the route of troops supplying the Court, and it also became the main center of production and supply for the Carioca market. This region was composed of large slave-holding properties essentially focused on internal supply. “The subsistence economy of South Minas Gerais, then, would have been established to supply the Province in the 18th century, maintaining the same nature of the regional economy by directing the surplus to the Rio de Janeiro market” (LENHARO, 1993).

At this time, as Rubião (1919) states, “communications from the interior of Brazil to the coastline were made through trails through the Mantiqueira mountains on roads winding through the Paraíba Valley, leading to the ports of Rio de Janeiro and Mangaratiba” (RUBIÃO, 1919, as quoted in SALES, 2003, p. 220). These communications, which took place over long periods of time, “in small caravans of gold prospectors and sesmeiros, became a source of life and development for the interior of the country” (RUBIÃO, 1919, as quoted in SALES, 2003, pp. 220-221).

And Rubião continues:
--- seg-71ed951041663911477e90b3 [needs-review] ---
As "troops" organized with mules coming from Campo Grande, through the emerging fairs of Sorocaba and Taubaté, flooded the country's interior roads, carrying salt and iron from the coast and bringing hides, cotton fabrics, and other goods. The cities of Campanha and Formiga became commercial centers. The small towns that mediated between the largest centers, planted along the road at that time, grew under the impetus of the troop movement that facilitated trade between these cities and the coast (Rubião, 1919, cited in Sales, 2003, p. 221).
--- seg-18da9e234fa0fd609826d0a9 [needs-review] ---
Andrade (2005) states that the mines in the Verde River, discovered by Paulistas in the first decades of the 1700s, were only made known in 1737 through a military expedition led by Cipriano José da Rocha, the Auditor of the Village of São João del Rei, following orders from the Governor of the Capitania, D. Martinho de Mendonça de Pina e Proença, to survey the region and explore the unknown lands along the Verde River basin.

===== PAGE 19 [page-0fe43cf1dee6241984e9d829] =====
--- seg-8455957069abe4547eb759a9 [needs-review] ---
Registration Process for intangible property: "The E.T. Case and the Varginha Identity"

Municipality of Varginha | MG

Year 2023 | Exercise 2025
--- seg-1fedfa9a35eb7d64c83b1094 [needs-review] ---
Sapucaí and Palmela took possession of the territory in the name of the king. From there, the settlement of Campanha emerged, and gradually, the entire region was taken over.

Although there were records indicating that the southern region had been visited in 1737, it was, in fact, 52 years later that it became an independent region. The southern region of Minas Gerais was emancipated from the municipality of Campanha, known as Campanha da Princesa, in 1789 (ANDRADE, 2005).
--- seg-8dc008cb2e144c7f657f3993 [needs-review] ---
Originally located in the Comarca do Rio das Mortes, which had seceded from the central mining region with the founding of its seat in São João D’El Rey in 1713, Campanha became, then, the seat of the Comarca do Rio Sapucaí in 1833. Almost naturally, the region that came to form the Comarca do Rio Sapucaí would have its boundaries delimited to the North by the Rio Grande and to the South and Southeast by the Mantiqueira mountain range. Thus, the cradle of Southern Minas is Campanha, a city that would be split off still in the first half of the 1800s into the cities of Baependy (1814), Jacuhy (1814), Pouso Alegre (1831), Lavras (1831), Jaguary (1840), and Itajubá (1848). (...) A regional dynamism that would be affirmed still in the first half of the 19th century, embodied in the separatist movements led by the city of Campanha in the last decades of the 19th century. A region whose determinants advance those existing in the urbanization of the mining region: agriculture and supply commerce shape its identity and vocation. (SAES, 2010:14;15)
--- seg-415618f0332f6ea1fbc8547e [needs-review] ---
The County of Rio das Mortes was administratively established in the Province of Minas in 1822, along with three other counties: Vila Rica, Serro, and Rio das Velhas. The name "Rio das Mortes" derives from the county's location beside a river of the same name, which had been known since the 17th century.

The three oldest towns in Southern Minas: Aiuruoca, Baependi, and Campanha, belonged to the ancient County of Rio das Mortes until 1833.

Andrade (2005) states that Campanha is considered the agglomerating core and the origin of Southern Minas. The parishes of Aiuruoca and Baependi were the first to be established, in 1718 and 1723, respectively.

The Campaign Term comprised ten parishes: Lavras do Funil, Baependi, Pouso Alto, Santa Ana do Sapucaí, Camanducaia, Ouro Fino, Itajubá, Cabo Verde and Jacuí, Carrancas, and Aiuruoca (ANDRADE, 2005).

In the first half of the 19th century, the Southern Minas region had commercial relations with the Empire; at the transition from the 19th to the 20th century, relations between this region and São Paulo were intensified. The South of the State of Minas Gerais would transition from a region producing commodities to one that became a major industrial center.

===== PAGE 20 [page-f3f28f5debdc37fb89d960ab] =====
--- seg-6e6a47ec21ad2d43493801d6 [needs-review] ---
Registration Process for intangible property: "The E.T. Case and the Varginha Identity"

Municipality of Varginha | MG

--- seg-13d6363abacbf9b1d73e7e3a [needs-review] ---
The region, oriented towards export activity thanks to coffee cultivation, received its railway in the last quarter of the 19th century, along with banks that only met small needs, as larger businesses were supplied by government sources or banks from Rio de Janeiro and São Paulo. It was during this period that capitalist commercial practices developed in the region, with the emergence of small factories and the intensification of the commercial sector.

The cities were characterized by being of medium size, with populations between ten and forty thousand inhabitants, but there were also many smaller towns. From 1890, an increase in the number of cities and the expansion of the railway network can be observed, which increased the region's export capacity to neighboring areas but also intensified competition from imported goods in local markets.

The South Minas region stood out as a coffee producer in the last quarter of the 19th century and throughout the 20th century. However, the same region was also responsible for a traditional agro-pastoral production oriented towards the domestic market, still at the end of the 18th century and the first half of the 19th century, a condition that contributed to South Minas becoming one of the most dynamic regions of the province/state during the period in question, initially due to a significant demographic condition that allowed great political influence between 1870 and 1920.

From these data, we can observe the demographic growth of South Minas cities. Between 1872 and 1920, the region presented the highest population growth rate in the state. In 1920, it was the region with the largest population in Minas Gerais, accounting for 20% of the state's population. In 1972, the region concentrated 260,000 inhabitants, a number that rose to 730,000 in 1907 and finally to approximately one million in 1920. Already when observing the cities individually in 1920, none of them (considering them as urban nuclei) had more than 20,000 inhabitants (GAMBI, 2012).

The introduction of coffee in the region at the end of the 19th century would lead to the development of new municipalities and the growth of existing ones where the culture flourished.

(...) along with the growth of the population (...) it was clear the increase in the number of cities. Alfenas, Boa Esperança, Três Corações, and Varginha, for example, were developing.

part of a first phase of municipal emancipation, between the 1860s and 1880s, as a result of the introduction of coffee in the region (SAES, 2010, p. 19).
--- seg-3f7c60172d6b95c22077168d [machine-unreviewed] ---
In the year 1911, the southern municipalities of Minas Gerais would increase, rising from 36 to 48. Thus, the southern region became more dynamic, expanding its economic activities in comparison to other regions of the state. However, out of these 48 municipalities, only 5 had a population between seven and twelve thousand inhabitants, 4 had a population between five and seven thousand inhabitants, and 3 had a population between four and five thousand inhabitants (GAMBI, 2012).
--- seg-183a87645c0b31b7c158051d [needs-review] ---
Unlike the Zona da Mata with Juiz de Fora, the central area from the 1920s with Belo Horizonte and Teófilo Otoni to the Vale do Mucuri, the South Minas region would be characterized by a deep fragmentation of its population into small and medium-sized cities, without a central irradiating center of economic activities (SAES, 2010, p. 19).
--- seg-1973969ac3deae4fadb5d3d6 [needs-review] ---
The railroads in southern Minas Gerais were constructed around 1880, but before that they had reached the Mata and the West. They came through four companies: Rio Verde Railroad, which later became known as Minas and Rio; Sapucaí Railroad; Muzambinho Railroad; and Mogiana Railroad Company, which originated in São Paulo and reached the southern region of Minas Gerais in the early years of the 20th century to serve the Triangle and the South. The lack of a central irradiating hub for transportation led, initially, to the integration of its railroads with external markets, rather than among the cities of the region themselves.
--- seg-a54846951c3f52cde7bfaf16 [needs-review] ---
Although provisionally, it is possible to think that the railways in Southern Minas Gerais began to be established to serve a dynamic and promising region focused on the supply trade to Rio de Janeiro and São Paulo, and for coffee planting and export. And more importantly, what is observed is the configuration of the railway system with interconnection of Southern Minas with the ports of Rio de Janeiro and Santos, or with the markets of neighboring states, a modernization of transport that could emerge as a condition to strengthen the internal market, but which, apparently, emerges to tighten relations, including dependencies, with other regions (SAES, 2010, p. 19).
--- seg-eef1d07ce5af2ad7cce465c2 [needs-review] ---
Then the first factories in Southern Minas Gerais emerged. Compared to those in the Mata region and the metalworking area, they were more rudimentary, often linked to supply or to

===== PAGE 22 [page-f684153e38d6c6abdc30ff93] =====
--- seg-b2d164cad1c5ed9d72062fc2 [needs-review] ---
Registration Process for intangible property: "The E.T. Case and the Varginha Identity"

Municipality of Varginha | MG

Year 2023 | Fiscal Year 2025
--- seg-9ac69d126f4e4ced90e9f9ac [machine-unreviewed] ---
The economy was agrarian-export-oriented, based on coffee, with an average of four employees per establishment. Additionally, South Minas industries were characterized by family-run businesses, such as crafts and food production, primarily for local consumption.
--- seg-318e4198530258f4911da7ba [needs-review] ---
Small and rudimentary manufactures had to compete with imported products from other states or abroad. The company flourished as a family business, archaic and distant from the characteristics of modern large industry. In this sense, the South of Minas became another specific case within the "mosaic" of Minas: a historically dynamic region, both for its function of supplying the imperial court and for its transformation due to the expansion of coffee plantations in the transition to the 20th century, but which was unable to keep up with the pace and economic vigor of neighboring states, Rio de Janeiro and São Paulo (SAES, 2010, p. 17).
--- seg-3c0c6b1ef0653396652d1a13 [needs-review] ---
In the 1920s, the southern region began expanding its industrial production, becoming the second-ranking sector, while the metallurgical region held the top position. This was due to the persistence of an archaic industrial structure in metallurgy and the greater representation of the southern industry in Minas Gerais' industrial economy. However, even though it had increased its industrial production, revealing greater representation in Minas Gerais' industrial output, and was in second place in the state's industrial sector, the southern Minas Gerais industry was still archaic, lacking a larger consumer market and limited accumulation capacity.

Regarding the banks in the region, the development of this sector mirrored the precarious industrial growth: although there was a significant expansion in the number of banks in southern Minas Gerais between 1910 and 1920, due to the coffee culture and the advent of railroads and industries, most banking operations were conducted in external banks in cities like Juiz de Fora (Real Credit Bank) and Belo Horizonte (Hipotecario e Agrícola Bank and Comércio e Indústria Bank). These three banks managed approximately 90% of Minas Gerais' current account deposits between 1920 and 1925. The banks in southern Minas Gerais were small and served the local community (COSTA, 1978).

Although the development of the southern region of the state, as the 19th century gave way to the 20th, might be considered precarious, it was during this time that capitalism in the region began to

it takes shape. It is important to note that during this transition, only three regions in the state of Minas Gerais underwent a transformation to the capitalist production system: the south, the Mata zone, and the center.

The transformations in the socio-economic relations through which the region passed during the transition from the 19th to the 20th century—the growth and development of cities, coffee cultivation, the emergence of industries, intensification of trade, development of railroads and banks—directed the region towards greater integration with external markets, fostering capitalist production relations.

Among the cities that emerged in the south of Minas Gerais with the expansion of the coffee culture in the region, as the 19th century gave way to the 20th, was Varginha, founded in 1882, initially called Espírito Santo das Catanduvas. In the next item, we will learn about the history of Varginha's development.
--- seg-3905b53a7d376803db6d5875 [needs-review] ---
2.2 Varginha Formation
--- seg-a103f8cc8ce6d2a9c9c5b1eb [needs-review] ---
The region where the municipality of Varginha is located was inhabited until the late 17th century by the Cataguás indigenous group. This ethnic group, whose name means "good people," is described by historians and archaeologists as a warrior and strong people of medium height, knowledgeable in ceramics, nomadic, and living in rudimentary shelters. Catanduvas (or Catandubas), a word of Tupi origin, was the name they gave to this region, meaning "spiny and low forest," common in these lands with clayey and infertile soil.

The Portuguese Crown, aiming to expand and occupy Brazilian territory and seek mineral riches (stones and precious metals), promoted in the 17th century exploration expeditions into the interior of Brazil—known as Entradas or Bandeiras. As these men, commonly called Bandeirantes, penetrated into the territory, they expelled or exterminated the original inhabitants of the region. From there, the first settlements or arraiais emerged for the supply and rest of the troops, led at various times by the bandeirante Fernão Dias Paes Leme (1608-1681), who would give his name to one of the main highways of Minas Gerais. It was within this context that Varginha emerged.
--- seg-c302dc431e73ea5949fd3e53 [needs-review] ---
Varginha Municipality | Mayor: Verdi Lúcio Melo | Sector Head: Danielle de Souza Guimarães
                     Responsible Sector: Cultural Heritage Technical Coordination
              Praça Matheus Tavares, 121 | Neighborhood: Center | Zip Code: 37002-320 | Tel.: (35) 3690-2718

The earliest references to Varginha date back to 1763, mentioning the Chapel of Saint Anthony of Rio Verde, located on the former road connecting Três Pontas to Campanha. It is likely built by bandeirantes (pioneer settlers) who traveled through the area. Around the chapel, landing ranches for travelers developed, leading to the growth of the settlement along the route of troops carried by mules from São Paulo, particularly from Sorocaba and Taubaté, who transported all sorts of goods to the Village of Campanha da Princesa da Beira, now known as Campanha.
--- seg-cabafaab09be2964696d81ba [needs-review] ---
The settlement was given the name Catandubas or Catanduvas, which means "Serrated Forest", a common feature in that area. Due to its patron saint, the chapel became known as Espírito Santo das Catandubas. In 1806, the village had a population of 1000 people. That same year, the territory that formed the original patrimony of the developing nucleus was donated. A year later, the village of Espírito Santo was officially established, with a small and modest agricultural village. In 1850, it was elevated to a parish (...) resulting in the neighborhood of Vargem, located 1 km (0.62 miles) northeast of the village (LEFORT, 1950).
--- seg-8a23af6ebce30afb507e6064 [needs-review] ---
According to Lefort (1950), we should consider the years 1790 to 1793 as the beginning
of the colonization of Varginha. It was during this period that many families from Campanha
and Lavras arrived with their enslaved people and built the first houses on the Santo Antônio
farm of Bom Jardim do Rio Verde, where the Santo Antônio chapel, active since 1763, was
located.
        The first documented record of the Varginha settlement dates back to 1795,
reporting the birth of Maria Francisca de Jesus, baptized in the Divine Spirit Santo chapel
in Catanduvas, as stated on page 45 of the 5th Book of Lavras from the Campaign M.G. Diocese.
--- seg-9dd048ee42150571a9f61b62 [needs-review] ---
On the third of April, one thousand seven hundred and ninety-five, at the chapel of the Holy Spirit of Catanduvas, a filial of the Matriz of Sant'Ana das Lavras do Funil, the Reverend Coadjutant Antônio José dos Santos baptized and anointed with holy oils Francisca, legitimate daughter of Joaquim Vitoriano de Andrade and Antônia Maria Felícia; Francisco Nogueira and Maria Francisca served as godparents, and I, as witness, signed the record. The vicar, José da Costa Oliveira. (LEFORT, 1950, as quoted in SALES 2003, pp. 118-119).

At that time, the settlement had approximately 1,000 people. In the same year, the territory that formed the original patrimony of the developing nucleus was donated. The following year, the Curate of Espírito Santo das Catandubas was established, located in the central region of what is now Varginha. For 43 years, the locale was a curate, a sort of village with the necessary conditions to become a district of a municipality. On June 1, 1850, the parish was elevated to a municipality through Provincial Law No. 471, being annexed to the municipality of Três Pontas. Varginha would then experience its first development boom. The first public buildings were constructed, including the first two public schools and the jail.

Spiritus Santus das Catanduvas would be transformed into the municipality of Espírito Santo da Varginha on September 22, 1881, by Law No. 2785. A municipality can be defined as an administrative division of a region, with administrative autonomy, possessing a City Hall and a Municipal Council.

On October 7, 1882, by Law No. 2950, the village of Espírito Santo da Varginha was elevated to a city. Regarding the creation of the municipality, Fonseca & Liberal (1920) report:

Regarding the urban formation and evolution of Varginha, Ávila (1983) clarifies:
--- seg-fa4b0c188f4b4265cb5e8abb [needs-review] ---
Varginha adhered, during its early stages of administrative autonomy and economic development between 1882 and 1892, to the standards still prevalent in Minas Gerais cities at the time. The road network developed parallel or perpendicular to the old main paths of the primitive village, resulting in a haphazard or circumstantial layout, in a process of expansion conditioned by the urgency or imposition of new constructions to house the growing population. The main road, along whose margin the first chapel was built between 1875 and 1880, and where the first six tiled houses were constructed between that year and 1820, corresponded to the road or simple dirt path connecting the Campaign Village to the then-called Three Points Backlands, and it is presumed to have cut diagonally through the current central perimeter of the city. This initial cluster of dwellings formed near where, much later, the Rio Branco Avenue (ÁVILA, 1983, apud SALES, 2003, pp. 224-225) would open.
--- seg-f5deef16ae2d555a3ae176b3 [needs-review] ---
Ávila (1983) presents a comparative analysis between the large urban voids created by Rio Branco Avenue and public squares and the denser core of the side streets:
--- seg-6c7fc63cd2b6f49e8c06d948 [needs-review] ---
It is worth emphasizing that throughout the urban history of the former Catanduvas settlement, the contrast between larger or more extensive voids and narrow circulation ways or dense residential concentration has always been a constant, voids that were almost always intended for the construction of more prominent buildings with community functions, as well as venues for festive events or leisure activities (...) the city, in reality, functioned as a kind of grand noble courtyard or veranda extending from the dozens of farms that surrounded it, at a close, medium or relative distance, an urbanized territory where rural owners and their eventual dependents traded their products, stocked their depots or pantries, communicated with national centers-of-gravity of the time – São Paulo and Rio de Janeiro – promoted the preparatory education of their children and participated in acts, events and rituals of political, social and religious convivence. (ÁVILA, 1983, quoted in SALES, 2003, p. 225).
--- seg-946316ae5553974e0abf6207 [machine-unreviewed] ---
The first more evident manifestation of the urban sectorization of Varginha, according to Ávila (1983), "is the one that begins to take shape around 1892, with the arrival of the railroad and the inauguration of the railway station, determining not only the expansion of the city into the lands bordering the railroad, but also the concentration of activities there."

In this context, according to Ávila (1983), it was possible to observe the social stratification

In 1890, the first Varginha newspaper, the Gazeta de Varginha, was inaugurated. In 1892,
the railroad reached the city: it was the Muzambinho Railroad, which arrived in the municipality
with the help of financial investments from the first mayor, Matheus Tavares da Silva. In
1892, the urban area had 13 streets, 6 squares, 2 public buildings (one for municipal functions,
such as the jail) and 3 churches (Matriz, São Sebastião, and Rosário). In 1899, Varginha was
experiencing a growing number of houses, and thus, the city began to be paved.

In 1902, public lighting with acetylene gas was inaugurated. In 1913, the Varginha
Telephonic Company was founded, with over 300 installed devices, and the municipal cemetery
was relocated, which had previously been located in Praça da Fonte, along with the demolition
of the old Rosário Church. Electric lighting was installed in 1914, and the municipal market was
opened in 1915. In the same year, the first bank in the city, the Banco Hypotecário Agrícola,
opened on Avenida Rio Branco, followed in 1918 by the Banco do Brasil, located in Praça Dom
Pedro II. These events demonstrate that the city was undergoing significant dynamization
towards modernization.

The establishment of a newspaper, the arrival of the railroad, the increase in streets and
houses, as well as the creation of cultural and educational institutions, are facts that show
the municipality's progress in its economic and social development. In 1918, the city had a
population of __UFO_PROTECTED_000__.

During its administrative development, Varginha belonged to the Rio das Mortes Comarca, a vast territory with imprecisely defined boundaries. It also briefly belonged to Lavras and later to the Três Pontas Comarca. Varginha also incorporated other districts: Elói Mendes, emancipated in 1911, and Carmo da Cachoeira, emancipated in 1938. The municipality, at the end of the 19th century and beginning of the 20th century, had elements that can be considered significant when it comes to the process of capitalist production mode development in a region. Below, we will discuss a bit about the importance of these factors in the formation of the Varginha municipality in the context of southern Minas Gerais.
--- seg-704a460dbe3e3f95446c9e0b [needs-review] ---
2.3 The municipality of Varginha: aspects of development in the 19th century and early 20th century
--- seg-68c0bc051d8347b147196d79 [machine-unreviewed] ---
Compared to other much older municipalities with stronger and more developed economies, such as Campanha, Varginha experienced its golden age, according to Sales (2003), between 1881 and 1913. Marked by social and economic development, Varginha became a regional expression center from its municipality and city creation in 1881 and 1882, the abolition of slavery in 1888, its elevation to county seat in 1890, the implementation of the railroad in 1892, and Italian immigration in the late 1800s and early 1900s, effectively determined Varginha's progress throughout the 1900s.

Initially, the municipality's economy was composed of commercial establishments, small industries, and banks. Varginha supplied tobacco, bacon, pigs, and cattle to the court, receiving ironworks, beverages, and salt in return. By 1907, the city exported coffee and dairy products.
--- seg-df1325081e91cce1cf6eeddc [needs-review] ---
Varginha Municipality | Mayor: Verdi Lúcio Melo | Sector Head: Danielle de Souza Guimarães
                     Responsible Sector: Cultural Heritage Technical Coordination
              Praça Matheus Tavares, 121 | Neighborhood: Center | Zip Code: 37002-320 | Tel.: (35) 3690-2718
--- seg-02186e284b5c0f5e1821229f [machine-unreviewed] ---

===== PAGE 30 [page-95cdf246cecd46593bb86c66] =====
--- seg-ec96fd0b534caad4f3382034 [needs-review] ---
Registration Process for intangible property: "The E.T. Case and the Varginha Identity"

Municipality of Varginha | MG

Year 2023 | Exercise 2025
--- seg-64bb246c83f60ab397827934 [needs-review] ---
In 1917, coffee became the main export product, alongside livestock. Wheat, salt, and textiles were the main imported goods.

After the decline of the mining economy, the coffee economy allowed Brazil to make significant strides toward consolidating capitalist production relations, marking the beginning of Brazilian industrial development. It was in the states of São Paulo and Rio de Janeiro that capitalism developed most rapidly due to the conditions that coffee provided.

Since 1870, the city of São Paulo had been experiencing intense economic growth, which, thanks to the support of coffee cultivation, led to the expansion of activities related to the formation of industries, railroads, and banks. These activities, which were an extension of coffee farming, developed because coffee created an economic complex in São Paulo, meaning that these other activities—banks, industries, trade, and railroads—arose because of coffee, to serve the coffee economy.

By the late 19th century, the southern region of Minas Gerais also received influences from the expansion of the coffee economy, stimulating urban growth in the area. Coffee was also responsible for the economic transformations that occurred in Minas Gerais, particularly in the Zona da Mata. However, the southern region of Minas Gerais also benefited from the advantages brought by coffee cultivation.

The dynamics generated by the coffee economy allowed railroads to develop in the south starting from the third quarter of the 19th century, facilitating the transport of goods and increasing the profits generated by coffee. Not only were railroads an extension of the coffee economy in southern Minas, but also industries (from 1889), trade, and the banking network (primarily from the 20th century).

Coffee farming in southern Minas expanded to the municipalities of Aiuruoca, Jacuí, and Baependi in the Sapucaí River Valley by the late 18th century (FILETO; ALENCAR, 2001). In the second half of the 19th century, coffee gained more space to the north and west, reaching Lavras, Nepomuceno, Perdões, Bom Sucesso, and Campo Belo. To the east, coffee plantations advanced into the areas of Monte Santo de Minas, São João da Fortaleza (now Arceburgo), Santa Bárbara das Canoas (Guaranésia), and Cabo Verde. All of these were established as extensions of the westward expansion of São Paulo (OLIVEIRA; GRIMBERG, 2007).
--- seg-c469ca368a10ab26cef12085 [needs-review] ---
Varginha Municipality | Mayor: Verdi Lúcio Melo | Sector Head: Danielle de Souza Guimarães
                     Responsible Sector: Cultural Heritage Technical Coordination
              Praça Matheus Tavares, 121 | Neighborhood: Center | Zip Code: 37002-320 | Tel.: (35) 3690-2718
--- seg-4f8b865c0c9594f96d00a7c0 [machine-unreviewed] ---
Page 30 of 403

===== PAGE 31 [page-c355ce056b850beb84c6e31f] =====
--- seg-20c1919b36ec278602db9859 [needs-review] ---
Registration Process for intangible property: "The E.T. Case and the Varginha Identity"

Municipality of Varginha | MG

Year 2023 | Exercise 2025
--- seg-d4529d84cbe0d8141f0c3e2b [needs-review] ---
Throughout the second half of the 19th century, coffee cultivation began to leave the Paraíba Valley and expand into the old western regions of São Paulo (Campinas) and, a few years later, into the new western regions (Ribeirão Preto) with large plantations. There were vast areas of fertile land and good topography. In the province of São Paulo, slave labor was being replaced by European immigration, primarily from Italy, due to the Law of Free Womb and constant abolitionist movements. In the same context, the rapid expansion of the railway network greatly facilitated the disposal of productions (SEMEGHINI, 1991).

Regarding slave labor, Lefort (1950) states that in 1871, Minas Gerais had 340,000 enslaved people. Of these, 1,427 lived in Varginha, with 725 men and 702 women.

According to Rubião (1919), the year 1888, the date of the liberation of the captives, found the municipality of Varginha in the most complex period of its economic development. The author states, “the transformation of the old sugar cane cultivation, which was then carried out on a large scale in the municipality of Varginha, at the expense of our forests, brutally sacrificed in the furnaces of the mills, into the new coffee cultivation, which required a chosen and numerous workforce, was taking place slowly in an atmosphere of doubts and uncertainties” (RUBIÃO, 1919, as quoted in SALES, 2003, p. 166).

On the eve of the abolition of slavery, the economic, political, and social elite of Varginha was tense and insecure about the fate of their most valuable and lucrative assets. In this sense, a minute of the Municipal Chamber, at a session on April 20, 1888, proposes an appeal to the farmers of the municipality to gather to discuss Varginha's economic future.
--- seg-f9f846dc3e724a13af1cec33 [needs-review] ---
The President proposed that, given the abnormal state of relations between slaves and their masters, and fearing a disaster for agriculture due to the immigration of slaves to areas where they are more protected in the pursuit of freedom, this Chamber address all slaveholders and municipal administrators a call for a meeting in this city on May 13 of the current year to discuss the transition from servile to free labor, and that this appeal be published in the press (MINUTE OF THE MUNICIPAL CHAMBER OF VARGINHA, 1888).
--- seg-6509a3fd3e1f7c2b40568c08 [needs-review] ---

===== PAGE 32 [page-454756e4fad848f83e33b997] =====
--- seg-fe34b987661b91d1a33d8311 [needs-review] ---
Registration Process for intangible property: "The E.T. Case and the Varginha Identity"

Municipality of Varginha | MG

Year 2023 | Exercise 2025
--- seg-62285ef53d10bfb2731f9b05 [needs-review] ---
The President of the Chamber, whose role was equivalent to that of a mayor, Major Evaristo Gomes de Paiva, was aware of the turbulent situation unfolding in the city, a result of abolitionist struggles and the same unrest that plagued Brazil as a whole. The atypical relationships between enslaved people and their owners revealed the desire of the former to break free from their bonds. In the face of the inevitable, the local elite gathered to discuss measures to transition from servile to free labor with minimal damage to the municipal economy and, consequently, to their own.

Due to the understanding of the imminent end of slavery, farmers, through the Municipal Chamber that actually represented them, sought to replace slave labor with European immigration, primarily bringing Italians to the Varginhense plantations. This action was a purely pragmatic measure, based on an exclusively economic paradigm.

In the final decades of slavery, the number of captives in Varginha was significant, organized primarily in the plantations as mentioned earlier. However, even before the abolition, many farmers in the municipality had already freed their slaves, as in some farms in the southern region of Minas (SALES, 2003, p. 168). The fact that the meeting was scheduled ten days before the end of the slave institution demonstrates the attachment to the servile institution and the economic elite's desperation over the loss of their productive goods. Post-abolition, however, the minutes make no mention of the situation of the now-freed black population in Varginha.

According to Luiz José Álvares Rubião, a memorialist from the early 1900s, the replacement of sugar cane with coffee, which the Varginhense plantations underwent, requiring intense and numerous labor forces, occurred slowly amidst a landscape of doubts and uncertainties. Thus, new labor was needed to take over the tasks of the slaves, who were already scarce in the city. He stated that Italian immigration had saved the plantation, as well as Varginha's economy.

SALES (2003) highlights that the black race had a decisive influence on Varginha's development, particularly with the introduction of coffee farming before the onset of Italian immigration (SALES, 2003, p. 167).

In fact, the influence of enslaved and later freed individuals on the economic development of Varginha, now an important agricultural and industrial center in southern Minas Gerais, was decisive, particularly in the trade and export of coffee to other countries.

Varginha followed the city of São Paulo in both economic growth and coffee production, and relied on Italian immigration to achieve progress by the late 19th century. Starting in 1888, the wave of immigration that brought foreigners, primarily from Italy, to Brazil reached Varginha, stemming from São Paulo. The Italians then took over coffee plantations, becoming merchants, landowners, industries, etc.

Southern Minas Gerais was an area rich in fertile and cheap land, and it became sought after as suitable terrain to start coffee farms on its territory.

According to Oliveira and Grinberg:
--- seg-a95296bf38c1c59701a70865 [machine-unreviewed] ---
(...) in some cases, they were families of farmers simply looking for new lands.
In others, they were relatives of commission or bank house owners. Since the lands were cheap, some Italian immigrants who originally worked as colonists, after saving some capital and relying on relatives, became landowners. Other farmers started as drovers or carters, transporting coffee bags on donkeys. Later, they invested in lands, became producers, but some did not stop trading, precisely to finance their own agricultural activity. Similarly, employees of commission houses, in charge of buying coffee directly from farmers, saved and invested in lands, and became producers. (OLIVEIRA; GRIMBERG, 2007, p. 20).
--- seg-25a99049c25b01c22e1b07af [needs-review] ---
Therefore, abundant and low-cost fertile land, idle labor force, expansion of railway lines, and most importantly, a long period of attractive coffee prices, explain the rapid expansion of coffee farming in Southern Minas.

Until that time, nearly all the coffee exported was produced in the Zona da Mata. From the late 1980s, coffee cultivation intensified in the South of Minas (...); with this, the weight of the South Minas product in the total export increased. By the end of the Empire, most of the exported coffee came from the Zona da Mata.

Coffee brought with it electricity, the automobile, and the telephone, fine fabrics, paved streets, and the construction of theaters and other "novelties." Therefore, the economy of southeastern Brazil at the end of the 19th century was marked by the coffee expansion, accompanied by a progressive ideology modeled after France that would determine the paths of progress and civilization. Varginha was not immune to this process, especially when coffee began to expand in the southern Minas Gerais region and slowly became its main product at the beginning of the 20th century.

It was during the expansion of the coffee economy in southern Minas Gerais that the city of Varginha developed, initially known as Espírito Santo da Varginha, having been emancipated from Três Pontas in 1881.

Varginha's main economic activities were trade and coffee cultivation. Coffee cultivation was introduced in the municipality around 1870 (RUBIÃO, 1919 apud SALES, 2003), but gained greater economic prominence from the 20th century onward, and it was common to hear praise for the development of the culture in Varginha's soil, as seen below:

The country, with the valorization of lands near railroads, the decline of towns and more remote regions, the imposition of monoculture, and the promotion of urbanization, among other notable aspects (Semeghini, 1991).

Thus, the arrival of the railroad represented a true transformation in the economy of Southern Minas. In the production process, it allowed for increased profit margins by reducing freight costs and eliminating losses. The railroad also facilitated the movement of labor, an essential requirement in the formation of the labor market.

The emergence of the railroad in Varginha, at the end of the 19th century, was a highly significant milestone in the economic development of the municipality and the southern region of Minas Gerais.

The railroad reached the city in 1892. Thus, the distances between Varginha and other municipalities would be traveled faster, thanks to the modernity of the time, which included financial investments from Matheus Tavares da Silva for its inauguration. The then-president of the City Council and owner of the greatest wealth in Varginha during the period of 1882 to 1883, a merchant, trader, owner of vast land extensions, and holder of many slaves (as indicated by the text), passed away in 1905, leaving behind two children and five grandchildren. Regarding the railroad, Sales (2003) states:
--- seg-1b2b525570bbc612d4b6f228 [needs-review] ---

According to Sales (2003), the Italian immigrants began a process of miscigeneration with the local population of Varginha, leading to profound changes in local customs and culture.

Coffee, a product of immense importance to the municipality's economy, was directly linked to industry. "Among the main industrial establishments, those dedicated to the processing and re-processing of coffee stand out, all located in close proximity to Varginha's railway station" (Rubião, 1919, as cited in Sales, 2003, p. 280). Other notable producers included those of: brandy, sugar cane, butter, and mechanical workshops.

2.4 Varginha: Economic Aspects (1930 - 2012)
--- seg-ba72607933a5d32f7b0ceced [needs-review] ---
Varginha boasts a privileged and strategic location, situated on the shores of Lake Furnas, and at the same time equidistant from three major Brazilian capitals: São Paulo, Rio de Janeiro, and Belo Horizonte.

Historically, agricultural activities have been firmly established in the municipality, with coffee farming playing a pivotal role in the local economy. The town features numerous warehouses, farmer cooperatives, and other forms of business organization, highlighting Varginha's significance in the national economy.

Given its geographic position and ease of access to raw materials and product distribution, the municipality hosts industries in various sectors, with a focus on agricultural processing and transformation, including the presence of multinational companies. The revenue generated by agriculture and industry fuels the local economy, encouraging investment in diverse commercial and service activities.
--- seg-412a4c5074ccf6423bf4f120 [needs-review] ---
Varginha Municipality | Mayor: Verdi Lúcio Melo | Sector Head: Danielle de Souza Guimarães
                     Responsible Sector: Cultural Heritage Technical Coordination
              Praça Matheus Tavares, 121 | Neighborhood: Center | Zip Code: 37002-320 | Tel.: (35) 3690-2718
--- seg-c737628f8e0e8e43d06301fe [machine-unreviewed] ---
Page 38 of 403

===== PAGE 39 [page-9f4e46204096fe432acff9df] =====
--- seg-fb426c857ee1083bfedbdbe6 [needs-review] ---
Registration Process for intangible property: "The E.T. Case and the Varginha Identity"

Municipality of Varginha | MG

Year 2023 | Fiscal Year 2025
--- seg-18dba5e9326b499fb201e158 [needs-review] ---
Brazilian society experienced a significant process of socio-cultural and economic transformations in the 20th century, evolving from an agrarian society to one with increasingly industrial and urban characteristics. (MARTINE et al., 1989; MARTINE, 1994). Since the 1930s, with the decline of the coffee agro-export model and the country's rapid urbanization due to industrialization, the relationships between rural and urban areas have been intensely transformed by the dynamics of capitalist production conditions.

The Great Depression (1929-1933) inflicted substantial losses on the coffee industry and, consequently, the entire Brazilian economy (combined with the issue of overproduction), as it became subject to external market conditions, leading to an awareness of Brazil's dependence on coffee policy, which was largely focused on the international market, or the vulnerability of an agro-export economy.

At the onset of the crisis, the economy stagnated across sectors, but from 1933, it began to grow again, particularly in the industrial sector. Agriculture also recovered, albeit slowly. As industries solidified, the national society started consuming internally produced goods, replacing imports. This led many coffee growers to abandon their large plantations and invest in other sectors, primarily industry. Many of these farmers changed their course because the coffee market was highly vulnerable.

However, the 1930s was a crucial period for Brazil's development, as what existed before were small factories, and it was only in the 1930s that the process of Brazilian industrialization began.

In this context, consumer markets shrunk dramatically. In Varginha, the situation was no different. Facing the crisis, coffee growers, as usual, sought support from the federal government, but it was unable to continue the protectionist policy for the sector.

Indeed, the 1929 crisis was also a significant factor in weakening the political power of coffee oligarchies, and it made it clear to dominant elites that the agro-export economy model was unsustainable and had limitations.
--- seg-dba6cadde5f70b0e6954bd10 [needs-review] ---
Varginha Municipality | Mayor: Verdi Lúcio Melo | Sector Head: Danielle de Souza Guimarães
                          Responsible Sector: Cultural Heritage Technical Coordination
                 Praça Matheus Tavares, 121 | Neighborhood: Center | Zip Code: 37002-320 | Tel.: (35) 3690-2718

===== PAGE 40 [page-b7fde285607205cf12bafdf1] =====
--- seg-088b8fef10d8894d01454daf [needs-review] ---
Registration Process for intangible property: "The E.T. Case and the Varginha Identity"

Municipality of Varginha | MG

Year 2023 | Fiscal Year 2025
--- seg-5dcd897f465ff565648a4892 [needs-review] ---
Over time, the coffee industry (processing and export) surpassed production (farming) in the city.
Varginha begins to expand. New neighborhoods emerge. The first, Vila Barcelona, was mostly inhabited by workers.
In the 1950s, according to estimates by the State Department of Statistics of Minas Gerais, coffee production represented 85.84% of the municipality's total agricultural output. Livestock farming was well-developed, particularly bovine livestock, which accounted for 76.84% in terms of herd value. Industry played a prominent role in the municipality's economic life, mainly manufacturing and factory industries, with 73.88% of the employed capital. It was therefore observed that the base of Varginha's economy was agriculture, followed by manufacturing and factory industries.
At that time, the city was still restricted to the "core" of the center. Houses ended at Avenida Major Venâncio, "Areião" (Fátima), Vila Barcelona, and Três Bicas. Neighborhoods like Catanduvas, Jardim Andere, Bom Pastor did not exist; they were considered rural areas.
The industrial development Varginha underwent only significantly began in the mid-1950s with the installation of CBC Indústrias Pesadas S.A., initially named "Companhia Brasileira de Caldeiras" and "Companhia Brasileira de Caldeiras e Equipamentos Pesados," respectively, founded in September 1955. This was a landmark in Varginha's industrialization, though modest by the end of the 1950s. The company came to Varginha before the city had institutions to train workers, which became more notable in the 1960s and 1970s. It was the municipality's largest company, employing up to 3,000 people.
Regional branches of state and federal governments began to be established. The era of higher education started in 1965 with the first higher education institution (Faculdade de Filosofia, Ciências e Letras). In subsequent years, the Faculdade de Direito de Varginha, Faculdade de Ciências Contábeis e de Administração, Faculdade de Engenharia Mecânica (now defunct Fenva), and Fepesmig, which later became the Centro Universitário do Sul de Minas, were established. More recently, Unifenas and Unifal.
--- seg-af8c1cf69abd4d4a2717bb76 [needs-review] ---

===== PAGE 41 [page-628177d83dc2440b2e553177] =====
--- seg-6919b251c7788f7a0be7536f [needs-review] ---
Registration Process for intangible property: "The E.T. Case and the Varginha Identity"

Municipality of Varginha | MG

Year 2023 | Exercise 2025
--- seg-39e7cafefe94264ee5a170c6 [needs-review] ---
Starting in the 1970s, Varginha's industrialization process accelerated and modernization in coffee production advanced. The municipality's agricultural orientation gradually shifted towards industry and service provision. The same occurred with coffee, with roasting and export figures surpassing those of the municipality's crops, generating income for the city. This adaptation to the market and new times allowed Varginha to become the second-largest coffee trading center in the world, only behind Santos on the São Paulo coast.

Varginha's contemporary industrial park began to take shape with companies like Moinho Sul Mineiro, Café Bom Dia, Polo Film, Plavigor, FL Smidth, Heatmaster, CBC, and Cooper Standard.

In the 1990s, the growth of the southern and southwestern regions of Minas Gerais led to the establishment in Varginha, in 1993, of the Interior Customs Station (EADI) or South Minas Dry Port, the first of its kind to operate in Brazil. These stations were created in the 1990s by the Federal Revenue Secretariat to alleviate the flow of goods in ports, airports, and border points across the country. This resulted in faster clearance of goods as customs procedures are conducted near the establishments of importers/exporters.

According to the Federal Revenue, in 2012, Varginha's Dry Port exported approximately $119.641 million in goods.

Currently, the municipality is one of Brazil's main centers for coffee trade, export, and production. According to the IBGE (2010), Varginha's economy is primarily driven by the service and industrial sectors. The institute also reports that in 2012, Varginha had the largest GDP in the Southern Minas Region, around R$ 4,007,089,000.00, and one of the largest in the state. The tertiary sector accounts for 53.2% of the total economically active population, the secondary sector for 34.3%, and the primary sector for 12.5%. The municipality boasts a notable industrial park and healthcare network, serving as the main public service hub in Southern Minas.

2.5 UFOlogy and ufological studies
--- seg-0fc39a22592e11264005af36 [needs-review] ---
Ufology studies so-called UFOs - Unidentified Flying Objects, or, in English, Unidentified Flying Objects (UFOs), or, alternatively, Unidentified Aerial Objects (UAO) and any type of unidentified or officially described extraterrestrial movement or activity.Ufology is an independent investigation branch that does not constitute a scientific research field or employ scientific methods in its inquiries or descriptions.

The word ufology derives from the combination of the acronym UFO, from the English "unidentified flying objects", and the suffix -logia, from ancient Greek -λογία, meaning study or field of knowledge.

The acronym UFO was coined by Edward J. Ruppelt, a captain in the United States Air Force and head of the Blue Book Project. In his 1956 book The Report on Unidentified Flying Objects, Ruppelt stated that UFO was an official term he had created to replace the popular expression "flying saucer." In Brazil, the term ufology is widely used, although the acronym UFO is commonly replaced by OVNI, and not by the equivalent term in European Portuguese, ovnilogia.

The term "flying saucer" emerged in 1947, derived from "saucer." In July 1947, several newspapers reported sightings of what was initially referred to as a "flying saucer," "UFO," or "hovering saucer." "Flying saucer" was translated in Brazil as "Disco Voador," in Italian as "disco volante," in Spanish as "plato volador," and in French as "soucoupes volantes." The term is believed to have been coined by pilot Kenneth Arnold on June 24, 1947, who observed nine aircraft flying from north to south in the US sky at an altitude of 3,200 meters, appearing to be connected. He described the objects as resembling "saucer jets," made of a silvery metal that gleamed in the sun.

These strange objects were linked to alien aircraft, which were thought to have no connection to Earth. If there were spacecraft from another planet, they would certainly be crewed by beings from outside Earth, or "ET - Extraterrestrial."

and denied by American aviation authorities. The case was reported by businessman Ray
Dimmick, who allegedly first sighted the crashed aircraft and inspected both the aircraft
and the pilot. The aircraft was said to be about 14 meters in diameter and made of a material
that appeared to be aluminum.

Previously). The pilot is considered the author of the first report of a flying saucer sighting in the United States. The term flying saucer itself is an adapted translation from the English term "flying saucer" first used by the Y East Oregonian newspaper in its edition of June 25, 1947, when the paper published an interview with Kenneth Arnold's account that the vehicles he saw flew like a "saucer skipping across water." In Brazil, the official name for flying saucers became OVNI, an acronym for Unidentified Flying Object.

It is within this context that Ufology emerged, to study these unexplained phenomena from human logic.

One of the most famous cases in global Ufology is the Roswell Case or the Roswell Incident, which occurred in New Mexico, United States. In July 1947, a fragment of a balloon was reportedly recovered on a ranch near the city of Roswell, New Mexico, United States.

On July 8, 1947, Walter Haut, the spokesperson for Roswell Air Base commanded by Colonel William Blanchard, issued the following press release:
--- seg-e80b008bcf37e75f07316fdf [needs-review] ---
The various rumors about the flying saucer became a reality yesterday when the information section of the 509th Bomb Group, VIII Air Force, Roswell Military Airport, was fortunate enough to obtain a disk thanks to the cooperation of a local farmer and the Chavez County Sheriff's office. The flying object landed on a farm near Roswell during the previous week. As there was no telephone, the farmer kept the disk until he could contact the sheriff's office, which in turn notified Major Jesse A. Marcel, of the information section of the 509th Bomb Group. It was immediately arranged for the disk to be retrieved from the farmer's residence. Examined at Roswell Military Air Base, it was later turned over by Major Marcel to the proper authorities (Wikipedia, Roswell Incident)1.

Local newspapers' front pages carried news that the 509th Bomb Group of the then U.S. Air Force had taken possession of the wreckage of a flying saucer. The news caused a stir among Roswell residents. The next day, the U.S. Air Force denied it was a flying saucer and claimed it was merely the remains of a weather balloon.

However, the story spread worldwide, and to this day, Roswell hosts an Annual Ufology Festival. Thousands of people from around the globe visit Roswell to learn about the flying saucer and its crew. In 1979, Colonel Thomas J. DuBose reportedly told the press that the explanation of the weather balloon was a cover-up for the true story, and the wreckage found had been switched to show to the journalists.

However, even before the more famous Varginha appearance in 1971, a document from the National Archives' collection in Rio de Janeiro indicated the appearance of an oval-shaped flying object over the municipality. The report was drafted by the Ministry of Aeronautics, dated March 3, 1971. This document, which we attach below, states: "A few months ago, a significant portion of the population of Varginha was alerted to an oval-shaped object, predominantly silver, that not only circled the city but also hovered at certain points in the city's neighborhoods." According to the official document, "in Vila Mendes, on Rio de Janeiro Street, the strange object remained stationary near the roof of a residence, causing one resident to lose consciousness due to the deafening noise."

--- seg-74840e5c6955fd5abc72cf02 [needs-review] ---
In addition to the previous document, the National Archive, located in the city of Rio de Janeiro, holds a document collection named "Unidentified Flying Objects" (UFOs). According to data published by the National Archive itself, the UFO Document Fund (BR DFANBSB ARX) contains records produced by the Brazilian Air Force Command. It comprises 743 entries, including reports, questionnaires on occurrences, correspondence, photographs, drawings, videos, audios, and newspaper and magazine clippings related to objects observed in the Brazilian sky over the years. The earliest occurrence in the UFO Document Fund dates back to 1952, while the most recent is from 2016.

The National Archive also notes that it is important to remember that UFOs do not necessarily imply the existence of flying saucers or extraterrestrials. This term – UFO – is used to designate any flying object whose immediate origin has not been identified. They could be satellites, drones, comets, balloons, natural phenomena, or other similar objects.
